\begin{equation}\label{fam:eq:shear-retract}
 R\circ g(u)^*\circ J=A(u).
\end{equation}
If $g(u)^*$ were analytic at $u_0$, bounded linear composition with $R$ and $J$ would make $A$ analytic there.
For the last assertion, take the counterexample of Theorem~\ref{thm:main}, which is nonanalytic at zero.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:main} makes $E,D,F$, and hence the parameter space $\cL(E,D)$, Banach.
Thus even an analytic family of invertible operators, with analytic inverse, can induce a nonanalytic
family on alternating maps over a Banach base. Equation~\eqref{fam:eq:shear-retract} also makes the
obstruction precise: it is already visible after bounded linear extension and restriction of forms.

\begin{corollary}[failure over a Banach base]\label{fam:cor:banach-base-failure}
Let $K$ be a nontrivially normed field of characteristic $p>0$, let $k\ge p$, and let $E,F$ be Banach spaces as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:main}. Put $M=\cL(E,E)$, an analytic manifold with one chart, and $H=E\times E$. Let
$\mathcal E\to M$ be the trivial bundle with fiber $H$, equipped with the two global trivializations
\[
 \tau_0(u,z)=(u,z),\qquad \tau_1(u,z)=(u,g(u)z),
\]
where $g$ is the shear of Proposition~\ref{fam:prop:shear}, and let $M\times F$ be the trivial bundle with
fiber $F$. Then $\mathcal E$ and $M\times F$ are analytic normed vector bundles, but the alternating-map bundle
$\Alt^k(\mathcal E;M\times F)$ with the
transitions~\eqref{fam:eq:bundle-transitions} is not analytic: its transition between the charts induced by $\tau_0$
and $\tau_1$ is analytic at no point of $M$. Equivalently, $u\mapsto g(u)$ is an automorphism of $M\times H$ in
$\mathsf{VB}^\omega_K(M)$ whose fiberwise alternating action on $M\times\Alt^k(H;F)$ is not an analytic bundle morphism.
Thus Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-bundles} fails over this Banach base, both for objects and for morphisms.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The transition of $\mathcal E$ from $\tau_0$ to $\tau_1$ is the affine analytic family $g$, with affine analytic inverse
$u\mapsto g(-u)$. By~\eqref{fam:eq:bundle-transitions}, the induced transitions of the alternating bundle are pullbacks
by $g(\pm u)$, with the identity on $F$. Theorem~\ref{thm:main} makes $A^k_{E,E;F}$ analytic at no point, so
Proposition~\ref{fam:prop:shear} shows that $u\mapsto g(u)^*$ is analytic at no point. The same holds for
$u\mapsto g(-u)^*$ after the linear reparameterization $u\mapsto-u$. The morphism statement is the same computation
in the global product chart of $M\times\Alt^k(H;F)$.
\end{proof}

The proof uses Theorem~\ref{thm:main} only through Proposition~\ref{fam:prop:shear}. Hence, whenever $A^k_{E,D;F}$ fails to be analytic at some $u_0$, the same construction over $M=\cL(E,D)$, with fiber $D\oplus E$ and target bundle $M\times F$, gives analytic input bundles whose alternating-map bundle has a transition that is not analytic at $u_0$. Every failure of analyticity of the functor therefore appears as a failure for bundles. The corollary concerns the compatibility of the natural construction with the given analytic atlas, or
equivalently its functoriality. It does not assert that the trivial pointwise alternating-map bundle admits no
analytic structure, and the $C^n$ forms of the construction hold over every base for $n\le\infty$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:smooth-bundles}).
The Lean formalization proves the corresponding contradiction for Mathlib's existing alternating-bundle topology
(Appendix~\ref{app:lean}).

\subsection{The Laurent series counterexample}\label{ssec:laurent-counterexample}
We describe the Banach spaces behind Theorem~\ref{thm:main} and outline its proof. Appendix~\ref{app:laurent} gives
the details of the construction. Let
$\kappa$ be a finite field of characteristic $p$, let $k\ge1$ and $r\in(0,1)$, and let $K_1:=\kappa((X))$ carry the
absolute value $\abs a=r^{\ord a}$, so that $\abs X=r$. Put $E_1:=\linf(\N,K_1)$ with the supremum norm. The
projective exterior norm on $\Lambda^k_{K_1}E_1$ is
\[
 \norm\omega_\pi:=\inf\Bigl\{\sum_j\prod_{i=1}^k\norm{x_{j,i}}_\infty\ :\ \omega=\sum_jx_{j,1}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{j,k}\Bigr\},
\]
the infimum over finite decompositions of an ordinary sum rather than a maximum. Let $B$ be the completion. For
$k\ge2$, sums of unit wedges on disjoint blocks of coordinates have norms in $B$ growing linearly with the number of
blocks, so $B$ admits no equivalent nonarchimedean norm (Proposition~\ref{prop:nonultra}). The universal wedge map
$W_B\in\Alt^k_{K_1}(E_1;B)$ is $W_B(x_1,\dots,x_k)=x_1\wedge\dots\wedge x_k$, and for $a\in E_1$ we write
$D_a\in\cL(E_1,E_1)$ for multiplication by $a$, $D_ax=ax$.

By Proposition~\ref{prop:lift}, it suffices to exclude a bounded $k$\nobreakdash-linear lift of $A$. Let
$E_0=\kappa^\N\subseteq E_1$ be the $\kappa$\nobreakdash-subspace of sequences with entries in $\kappa$. When $k\ge p$, the
multiplier theorem over a finite field (Theorem~\ref{thm:FF}, Appendix~\ref{app:FF}) rules out a multilinear lift of
coordinatewise multiplication on $E_0$ that satisfies a polarized diagonal identity and whose values have exterior
support of uniformly bounded dimension. To prove the
following theorem, we evaluate a hypothetical bounded $k$\nobreakdash-linear lift of $A^{k,K_1}_{E_1,E_1;B}$ on multiplication
operators $D_{a_1},\dots,D_{a_k}$ and at $W_B$, with all $a_i$ and all arguments in $E_0$, and apply the
$\kappa$\nobreakdash-linear constant-coefficient map $\eta\colon B\to\Lambda^k_\kappa E_0$. Its support dimension is bounded on
bounded subsets of $B$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:supp}). This converts the norm bound on the lift into an algebraic
obstruction: the result is a finite-field lift of coordinatewise multiplication of the kind excluded by
Theorem~\ref{thm:FF} when $k\ge p$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:Psi}).
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:laurent}
Let $\kappa$ be a finite field of characteristic $p$, let $k\ge p$ and $r\in(0,1)$, and let $K_1=\kappa((X))$ with
$\abs X=r$. Let $E_1=\linf(\N,K_1)$, and let $B$ be the completion of $\Lambda^k_{K_1}E_1$ under the projective exterior
norm. Then $A^{k,K_1}_{E_1,E_1;B}$ admits no bounded $k$\nobreakdash-linear lift over $K_1$. Consequently it is analytic at no point of
$\cL(E_1,E_1)$.
\end{theorem}
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{app:laurent}. Theorem~\ref{thm:main} follows by ascent, with $\kappa=\F_p$. For any
$t\in K$ with $0<\abs t<1$, the closure of $\F_p(t)$ in the completion of $K$ is such a Laurent series field.
Ingleton's Hahn--Banach theorem and projective base change pass the obstruction to the completion, and density
passes it to $K$ itself (Appendix~\ref{app:ascent}). The linear growth of sums of unit wedges on disjoint blocks persists after base change, so the resulting target $F$, like
$B$, admits no equivalent nonarchimedean norm (Proposition~\ref{prop:nonultra}). Appendix~\ref{app:assembly}
assembles the proof.

\subsection{Multiplier families and \texorpdfstring{$c_0$}{c0} bases}\label{lau:ssec:multipliers}
The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:laurent} uses the hypothetical lift only on coordinatewise multiplication operators and
only at the single form $W_B$. Moreover, Theorem~\ref{thm:FF}(1) needs only finitely supported test vectors with
entries in $\{0,\pm1\}$. Both observations give a much smaller bad parameter space. Throughout this subsection
$\kappa$, $k$, $r$, $K_1$, $E_1$, $B$ and $W_B$ are as in Theorem~\ref{thm:laurent}, $c_0(\N,K_1)\subseteq E_1$ carries the
supremum norm, and $D_a\in\cL(E_1,E_1)$ denotes multiplication by $a$.

\begin{proposition}[multipliers on $c_0$]\label{lau:prop:c0-multipliers}
The homogeneous polynomial
\[
 \sigma\colon c_0(\N,K_1)\to\Alt^k_{K_1}(E_1;B),\qquad
 \sigma(a)(x_1,\dots,x_k)=W_B(ax_1,\dots,ax_k)=(ax_1)\wedge\dots\wedge(ax_k),
\]
has no bounded $k$\nobreakdash-linear lift with values in $\Alt^k_{K_1}(E_1;B)$. Consequently, for every
$u_0\in\cL(E_1,E_1)$, the map $a\mapsto A^{k,K_1}_{E_1,E_1;B}(u_0+D_a)(W_B)$ is analytic at no point of $c_0(\N,K_1)$. In
particular $a\mapsto A^{k,K_1}_{E_1,E_1;B}(u_0+D_a)$ is analytic at no point.
\end{proposition}
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{app:laurent}.

\begin{corollary}[diagonal transitions over a $c_0$ base]\label{lau:cor:diagonal-bundle}
Let $U$ be the open unit ball of $c_0(\N,K_1)$, an analytic manifold with one chart. Let $\mathcal V\to U$ be the
trivial bundle with fiber $E_1$ and the two global trivializations $\tau_0(a,z)=(a,z)$ and $\tau_1(a,z)=(a,D_{1+a}z)$,
and let $\mathcal B=U\times B$. Then $\mathcal V$ is an analytic normed vector bundle whose transitions are isometric
diagonal automorphisms of $E_1$. The alternating-map bundle $\Alt^k(\mathcal V;\mathcal B)$ is not analytic: the section
that is constantly $W_B$ in the chart induced by $\tau_1$ is, in the chart induced by $\tau_0$, the map
$a\mapsto W_B\circ(D_{1+a},\dots,D_{1+a})$, which is analytic at no point of $U$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $a\in U$ every coordinate satisfies $\abs{1+a_i}=1$, so $D_{1+a}$ is an isometry of $E_1$. Its inverse
$D_{(1+a)^{-1}}=\sum_{n\ge0}(-D_a)^n$ is a power series of radius one in $a$, since $D_a^n=D_{a^n}$ has norm at most
$\norm a^n$. Hence both transitions are analytic. If $\omega_a\in\Alt^k(\mathcal V_a;B)$ has coordinates $m_0(a)$ and
$m_1(a)$ in the two induced charts, then $m_0(a)=m_1(a)\circ(D_{1+a},\dots,D_{1+a})$. With $m_1\equiv W_B$, this is the
map of Proposition~\ref{lau:prop:c0-multipliers} with $u_0=\id$, which is analytic at no point.
\end{proof}

Thus the analyticity of a section of the alternating-map bundle depends on the analytic frame used to read it, at
every point of the base. This shows that the nonarchimedean hypothesis on the final value space $F'$ in
Corollary~\ref{fam:cor:c0-families} cannot be removed: $B$ is complete but admits no equivalent nonarchimedean norm
(Proposition~\ref{prop:nonultra}). It also contrasts with Corollary~\ref{fam:cor:l1-families}. On
$\ell^1(\N,K_1)$, with its ordinary sum norm, the same multiplier family is admissible; its sorted-word lift (Lemma~\ref{fam:lem:l1-polynomial}) has norm at
most $k!$. The test vectors above are finitely supported in both spaces, so only the norm on the parameter space
distinguishes them. This is a concrete analogue of Corollary~\ref{fam:cor:quotient-parameters}, with the bounded inclusion $\ell^1(\N,K_1)\to c_0(\N,K_1)$ in place of a quotient map.

\begin{corollary}[finite dimension is sharp for nonarchimedean bases]\label{lau:cor:dichotomy}
Let $K=\F_q((u))$, where $q$ is a power of $p$, with a $u$\nobreakdash-adic absolute value, and let $k\ge p$. Let $P$ be a
$K$\nobreakdash-Banach space admitting an equivalent nonarchimedean norm. Then the conclusion of
Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-bundles} holds over every analytic manifold modeled on $P$, with arbitrary normed fibers,
if and only if $P$ is finite-dimensional.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If $\dim P<\infty$, Lemma~\ref{lem:findim} supplies continuous coordinates and Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-bundles}
applies. Conversely, suppose $\dim P=\infty$. Remark~\ref{rem:spaces} allows us to apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:laurent} with $\kappa=\F_q$ and $K_1=K$. By Serre's theorem cited in Remark~\ref{rem:sorted}, $P$ is
topologically isomorphic to $c_0(I,K)$ for an infinite set $I$. Choosing a countable subset of $I$ gives bounded linear
maps $\iota\colon c_0(\N,K)\to P$ and $\pi\colon P\to c_0(\N,K)$ with $\pi\iota=\id$. On the open set
$V=\{x\in P:\norm{\pi x}<1\}$, take the bundle with fiber $E_1$ and transition $D_{1+\pi(x)}$, as in
Corollary~\ref{lau:cor:diagonal-bundle}, together with the trivial bundle with fiber $B$. If the induced transition were
analytic at $x_0\in V$, restriction to the affine subspace $x_0+\iota(c_0(\N,K))$ would make
$b\mapsto A^k_{E_1,E_1;B}(D_{1+\pi(x_0)}+D_b)$ analytic at zero. This contradicts
Proposition~\ref{lau:prop:c0-multipliers} with $u_0=D_{1+\pi(x_0)}$.
\end{proof}

The ordinary sum norm on $\ell^1$ is not equivalent to a nonarchimedean norm, so Corollary~\ref{fam:cor:l1-families}
does not conflict with this dichotomy. Over $\F_q((u))$, every counterexample must also use a target fiber outside
$\mathsf T_k(K)$; by Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete}, a Banach target fiber must then admit no equivalent nonarchimedean norm.
Thus the obstruction does not occur among bundles all of whose target fibers are nonarchimedean Banach spaces.

\begin{remark}[pullback of differential forms]\label{lau:rem:pullback}
The same computation shows that pullback along analytic maps does not preserve intrinsic
alternating-valued analyticity of $B$\nobreakdash-valued forms on infinite-dimensional bases.
Here regularity is measured in the alternating-map space in the displayed trivialization; ambient
multilinear-valued analyticity is preserved, as explained in \S\ref{sec:scalar-forms}. Let $f\colon E_1\times E_1\to E_1$ be the bounded bilinear map $f(x,y)=xy$, and let
$\omega_0\equiv W_B$ be the constant $B$\nobreakdash-valued $k$\nobreakdash-form on $E_1$. At $(x,y)$, the restriction of
$f^*\omega_0=W_B\circ(Df,\dots,Df)$ to vectors in $\{0\}\times E_1$ is $\sigma(x)$, because $Df(x,y)(0,h)=xh$. By
Proposition~\ref{lau:prop:c0-multipliers} and the lift criterion at points of $E_1$, its coefficient map
into $\Alt^k(E_1\times E_1;B)$ is analytic at no point of $E_1\times E_1$. In the same way, the chart change $(x,y)\mapsto((1+y)x,y)$ on
$\{(x,y):\norm y<1\}$ has derivative $\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1+y}&D_x\\0&\id\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, which is the
shear with $u=D_x$ along $y=0$. Hence the bundle of $B$\nobreakdash-valued differential $k$\nobreakdash-forms on this open
subset of $E_1\times E_1$ is not analytic for the atlas induced by the analytic structure. For scalar-valued forms the
target $K$ is spherically complete when $K=\F_q((u))$, so Main Theorem~(\ref{main:spherical}) makes the bundle of
differential $k$\nobreakdash-forms analytic over every base. For complete fields that are not spherically
complete, Theorems~\ref{thm:scalar-tangent} and~\ref{thm:scalar-pullback} give scalar analogues of both failures.
\end{remark}

\section{Scalar targets and analytic differential forms}\label{sec:scalar-forms}

The counterexample of Theorem~\ref{thm:main} uses an infinite-dimensional value space.
For differential forms, the relevant value space is the scalar field itself. The scalar
counterexample proved in Appendix~\ref{app:scalar} settles this case and separates two notions:
analytic sections of an alternating-map bundle, and alternating-valued analytic sections of
the multilinear-map bundle. The latter definition supports the usual differential calculus
even when the induced alternating-bundle atlas fails to be analytic.

Throughout this section, $K$ is a complete nontrivially normed field and value spaces $F$ are Banach.
Manifold model spaces may be arbitrary normed $K$\nobreakdash-spaces; analytic chart changes and manifold
maps are understood in Mathlib's $C^\omega$ sense, so their derivatives are analytic even when the
models are incomplete. The coefficient maps below take values in Banach spaces, where $C^\omega$
agrees with power-series analyticity. The counterexample manifolds have Banach models.
No characteristic or degree restriction is imposed unless explicitly stated.

\subsection{The scalar classification}

\begin{theorem}[universal scalar targets]\label{thm:scalar-classification}
For every $k\ge0$, the contravariant functor
\[
 \Alt^k(-;K)\colon\mathsf{Ban}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Ban}_K
\]
is analytic if and only if either $k!\ne0$ in $K$, or $K$ is nonarchimedean and spherically complete.
The same criterion holds with $\mathsf{Vec}_K^{\mathrm{op}}$ as its domain.
In particular, in characteristic $p>0$ and degree $k\ge p$, scalar-valued alternating maps
form an analytic functor precisely when $K$ is spherically complete.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Proposition~\ref{prop:factorial-positive} proves the assertion when $k!\ne0$.
For a nonarchimedean, spherically complete $K$, the result of
\S\ref{sec:theoremA} applies with value space $F=K$. Both positive arguments allow arbitrary
normed source spaces. Otherwise $K$ has characteristic $p>0$ with $k\ge p$, and is
nonarchimedean by Lemma~\ref{lem:ultra} but not spherically complete.
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-counter} supplies Banach spaces $E,D$ for which
$A^k_{E,D;K}$ is analytic at no point. This already disproves analyticity on
$\mathsf{Ban}_K^{\mathrm{op}}$.
\end{proof}

A nonzero finite-dimensional value space has the same universal-target criterion, by
Remark~\ref{rem:finite-target-reduction}. In the obstructed regime, every universal target has zero
continuous dual (Proposition~\ref{prop:universal-zero-dual}); this is a necessary condition only.

The obstruction requires large source spaces in a stronger sense than infinite dimension.
A normed space is \emph{of countable type} if a countable subset has dense linear span.

\begin{proposition}[countable-type scalar sources]\label{prop:scalar-countable}
Let $K$ be complete and nonarchimedean. If either $E$ or $D$ is of countable type, then
$A^k_{E,D;K}$ is analytic in every degree. Neither completeness nor a nonarchimedean norm
is required of $E,D$. In particular, the two spaces in
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-counter} must both fail to be of countable type.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The case $k=0$ is constant. For a normed space $V$, set
\[
 q_V(x)=\inf_{x=\sum_i x_i}\max_i\norm{x_i},
\]
where the decompositions use nonempty finite families, allowing a zero term. Scaling and concatenating them show that
$q_V$ is a nonarchimedean seminorm. Let $V^u$ be the completion of its separated quotient;
the canonical map $j_V\colon V\to V^u$ is contractive and has dense range.
Expanding a bounded scalar multilinear form $m$ along decompositions of each argument gives
\[
 \abs{m(x_1,\ldots,x_k)}\le\norm m\prod_a q_V(x_a).
\]
Thus scalar multilinear forms extend uniquely and isometrically to $V^u$, as do
alternating forms. Extension preserves alternation by density of tuples with repeated
entries, and commutes with permutations. Every bounded $u\colon V\to W$ induces
$u^u\colon V^u\to W^u$ with $\norm{u^u}\le\norm u$. Uniqueness on the dense image makes
$u\mapsto u^u$ bounded linear, and extension of forms intertwines precomposition by $u$ and $u^u$.

If $V$ is of countable type, so is $V^u$. By van der Put's basis theorem
\cite[Proposition~1.4]{VdP}, it has a $t$\nobreakdash-orthogonal basis $(b_i)$ for any
fixed $0<t<1$. Writing $f_i$ for its coordinate functionals, define
\[
 S(m)(x_1,\ldots,x_k)
 =\sum_{i_1<\cdots<i_k}m(b_{i_1},\ldots,b_{i_k})
                        \prod_{a=1}^k f_{i_a}(x_a).
\]
The weighted coordinates $\abs{f_i(x)}\norm{b_i}$ form a null family bounded by
$t^{-1}\norm x$. Products of finitely many bounded null families are null, so the
displayed sum converges unconditionally in $K$ and $\norm{S(m)}\le t^{-k}\norm m$.
Let $\mathcal A_V$ be unnormalized antisymmetrization, the signed sum over permutations
of the arguments. It is bounded and alternating-valued in every characteristic.
On finite-coordinate vectors, sorting distinct indices shows
$\mathcal A_V S(m)=m$ for alternating $m$; repeated indices contribute zero.
Density proves the same identity everywhere. Hence $R=\mathcal A_V S$ retracts all
multilinear forms onto alternating forms, while $S|_{\Alt}$ is a bounded right inverse
of $\mathcal A_V$. Transport through $j_V$ gives both operators on the original $V$.

If $E$ is of countable type, a lift of $A$ is
$R_E\bigl(m\circ(u_1,\ldots,u_k)\bigr)$.
If $D$ is of countable type, use instead
$\mathcal A_E\bigl(S_D(m)\circ(u_1,\ldots,u_k)\bigr)$.
Both are bounded multilinear in the $u_i$, linear in $m$, and have diagonal $A(u)(m)$;
the second identity uses that antisymmetrization commutes with simultaneous precomposition.
Proposition~\ref{prop:lift} concludes the proof.
\end{proof}

\subsection{From operators to the tangent bundle}

The shear can be realized as a change of coordinates on the base manifold itself. This
strengthens the bundle obstruction of Proposition~\ref{fam:prop:shear} to a statement about
scalar differential forms.

\begin{theorem}[failure of the induced tangent atlas]\label{thm:scalar-tangent}
Suppose $K$ has characteristic $p>0$, is not spherically complete, and $k\ge p$.
There are Banach spaces $E,D$ such that the analytic Banach manifold
\[
 X=\cL(E,D)\times D\times E
\]
has two global analytic charts whose induced operator-valued transition family on
$\Alt^k(TX;K)$ is analytic at no point. One may take the charts to be $\id_X$ and
\[
 \psi(u,d,e)=(u,d+ue,e).
\]
Consequently the natural alternating-bundle construction does not preserve analytic
tangent-bundle atlases over all Banach bases under completeness of $K$ alone.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose $E,D$ from Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-counter}. Operator evaluation is bounded bilinear,
so $\psi$ and $\psi^{-1}(u,d,e)=(u,d-ue,e)$ are polynomial analytic maps.
The space $X$ is Banach, since $D$ is complete, and
\[
 D\psi(u,d,e)(v,a,b)=(v,a+ve+ub,b).
\]
Let $\pi_D\colon X\to D$ be projection and $i_E(b)=(0,0,b)$. The fixed bounded linear maps
$Jm=m\circ\pi_D^{\times k}$ and $Rn=n\circ i_E^{\times k}$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar-tangent-compression}
 R\circ A^k_{X,X;K}(D\psi(u,d,e))\circ J=A^k_{E,D;K}(u),
\end{equation}
because $\pi_DD\psi(u,d,e)i_E=u$. Analyticity of the induced transition at any
$(u_0,d_0,e_0)$ would, after this bounded linear compression and restriction to
$u\mapsto(u,d_0,e_0)$, make $A^k_{E,D;K}$ analytic at $u_0$.
For the reverse transition the same identity uses $-u$ instead of $u$.
Both conclusions contradict the choice of $E,D$.
\end{proof}

The obstruction concerns the atlas induced from the analytic base charts. It does not
assert that the pointwise alternating bundle admits no analytic structure: the identity
chart alone identifies it with the analytic product
$X\times\Alt^k(X;K)$. What fails is compatibility of that structure with the other induced
chart. Thus the two-chart atlas above also prevents an unconditional
\texttt{ContMDiffVectorBundle $\omega$} theorem for the standard construction, whose
regularity assertion concerns all transitions in the given bundle atlas.

\subsection{An intrinsic pullback obstruction}

The failure is visible on individual forms as well as operator-valued transitions.
Here an \emph{intrinsically analytic} form on an open subset of a Banach space $V$ means
an analytic map into the normed space $\Alt^k(V;K)$, using the standard product
trivialization.

\begin{theorem}[analytic pullback need not preserve intrinsic analyticity]
\label{thm:scalar-pullback}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-tangent}, there are Banach spaces $Y,N$,
an intrinsically analytic scalar $k$\nobreakdash-form $\omega_0$ on $Y$, and a polynomial
analytic map $h\colon N\to Y$ such that $h^*\omega_0$ is intrinsically analytic at no point.
Its image in $\Mult^k(N;K)$ is nevertheless analytic everywhere.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Keep the spaces $E,D$ above and put $H=\Alt^k(D;K)$,
$Y=H\times D$, and $N=\cL(E,D)\times H\times E$. Define
\[
 \omega_0(m,d)=m\circ\pi_D^{\times k},\qquad h(u,m,e)=(m,ue).
\]
The coefficient map of $\omega_0$ is bounded linear into $\Alt^k(Y;K)$, and $h$ is
polynomial. Since $Dh(u,m,e)(v,a,b)=(a,ve+ub)$, restriction of $h^*\omega_0$ to the
fixed $E$-directions $(0,0,b_i)$ gives
\[
 \Gamma(u,m)=A^k_{E,D;K}(u)(m)\in\Alt^k(E;K).
\]
If the pulled-back form were intrinsically analytic at $(u_0,m_0,e_0)$, bounded linear
restriction of its values and the slice $e=e_0$ would make $\Gamma$ analytic at
$(u_0,m_0)$. Its partial derivative in the $H$-variable is
\[
 D_m\Gamma(u,m)=A^k_{E,D;K}(u).
\]
Derivatives of analytic maps into Banach spaces are analytic in operator norm; restricting
this derivative to $m=m_0$ contradicts the nonanalyticity of $A$ at $u_0$.
Finally, after forgetting alternation, the coefficient map of $h^*\omega_0$ is a bounded
multilinear expression in $Dh$ and $\omega_0\circ h$. It is therefore analytic.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Ambient analytic forms}

The preceding example suggests a definition that is stable under analytic pullback.
For a chart $\phi\colon U\to V\subseteq P$, write $\omega_\phi$ for the coordinate
representative of a family $\omega_x\in\Alt^k(T_xM;F)$, and let
$j\colon\Alt^k(P;F)\hookrightarrow\Mult^k(P;F)$ be the isometric inclusion.

\begin{definition}
An \emph{ambient analytic $F$-valued $k$\nobreakdash-form} is such a family for which
$j\omega_\phi\colon V\to\Mult^k(P;F)$ is analytic in every chart.
Write $\Omega^k_{\mathrm{amb}}(M;F)$ for these forms.
\end{definition}

The multilinear-map bundle $\Mult^k(TM;F)$ is always analytic: its coordinate changes
are bounded multilinear expressions in the derivatives of the base chart changes.
Thus the definition says exactly that $\omega$ is an analytic section of this bundle
whose values are alternating. In a change of coordinates $\psi$,
\[
 j\omega_\phi(y)
 =\bigl(j\omega_{\phi'}(\psi(y))\bigr)\circ(D\psi(y),\ldots,D\psi(y)).
\]
The right-hand side is analytic by bounded multilinear composition, so it suffices to
check any one atlas. In degree zero the definition gives ordinary analytic $F$-valued
functions.

\begin{proposition}[calculus of ambient analytic forms]\label{prop:ambient-calculus}
Ambient analytic forms form a sheaf of modules over analytic scalar functions and are
preserved by analytic pullback. There are natural shuffle products, for every bounded
bilinear $\beta\colon F_1\times F_2\to F_3$, and exterior derivatives
\[
 d\colon\Omega^k_{\mathrm{amb}}(M;F)\longrightarrow
       \Omega^{k+1}_{\mathrm{amb}}(M;F).
\]
They satisfy $d^2=0$, the Leibniz rule, and $h^*d=dh^*$.
For scalar forms, the product is associative and graded commutative, so
$\Omega^\bullet_{\mathrm{amb}}(-;K)$ is a contravariant sheaf of differential graded
$K$\nobreakdash-algebras. These assertions hold in every characteristic.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Locality gives the sheaf property; addition and multiplication by analytic scalar functions
are bounded bilinear operations in each chart. For an analytic map $h$, the coordinate
formula $h^*\eta=(\eta\circ h)\circ(Dh,\ldots,Dh)$ is likewise analytic in the ambient
multilinear space. The chain rule proves functoriality of pullback.

For $S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k+l\}$, let $v_S$ list its entries in increasing order and put
$\epsilon(S)=(-1)^{\#\{(s,t):s\in S,\ t\notin S,\ s>t\}}$. Define
\[
 W(\mu,\nu)(v_1,\ldots,v_{k+l})
 =\sum_{\abs S=k}\epsilon(S)\,
   \beta\bigl(\mu(v_S),\nu(v_{S^c})\bigr).
\]
This is a bounded bilinear map on ambient multilinear spaces, with norm at most
$\binom{k+l}{k}\norm\beta$. For alternating $\mu,\nu$, it is alternating: if two
arguments coincide, terms placing them in the same block vanish; the remaining terms
pair by exchanging the two indices and have opposite signed values. This cancellation
also holds in characteristic two. The formula commutes with simultaneous precomposition,
so it defines the wedge product in charts and commutes with pullback. For scalar values,
complementing $S$ gives graded commutativity. Expanding either bracketing of a triple
product gives the same signed sum over ordered three-block partitions, proving
associativity.

On an open subset of a model space $P$, let $\eta$ be an ambient analytic representative.
For $T\in\cL(P,\Mult^k(P;F))$, define the bounded linear operator
\[
 \widetilde A(T)(v_0,\ldots,v_k)
 =\sum_{i=0}^k(-1)^iT(v_i)(v_0,\ldots,\widehat v_i,\ldots,v_k),
 \qquad d\eta=\widetilde A(D\eta).
\]
Its norm is at most $k+1$, so $d\eta$ is ambient analytic. The alternating subspace is
closed, being an intersection of kernels of continuous evaluations on repeated arguments.
Difference quotients therefore show that $D\eta(y)v$ is alternating. If $v_a=v_b$ with
$a<b$, all terms of $d\eta$ except $i=a,b$ vanish; moving the repeated vector across
the $b-a-1$ intervening positions shows that the remaining two terms cancel.
Thus $d\eta$ is alternating-valued.

The second derivative of an analytic map is symmetric over every nontrivially normed
field: for a local expansion with quadratic coefficient $p_2$ it is
$(v,w)\mapsto p_2(v,w)+p_2(w,v)$. In the expansion of $d(d\eta)$, the terms differentiating
in the ordered directions $(v_a,v_b)$ and $(v_b,v_a)$, $a<b$, have signs
$(-1)^{a+b-1}$ and $(-1)^{a+b}$ and otherwise agree. Hence $d^2\eta=0$.

To verify coordinate invariance, differentiate
$\psi^*\eta=\eta(\psi(y))\circ(D\psi(y),\ldots,D\psi(y))$.
The terms differentiating $\eta$ contribute $\psi^*(d\eta)$ after applying
$\widetilde A$. The other terms insert $D^2\psi(v_a,v_b)$ into one slot of $\eta$.
Pair the terms indexed by $(a,b)$ and $(b,a)$: symmetry of $D^2\psi$ and alternation of
$\eta$ cancel them, with the same signs as above. This proves
$d(\psi^*\eta)=\psi^*(d\eta)$, both for chart changes and for arbitrary analytic maps.
The local exterior derivatives consequently glue.

Finally differentiate the shuffle formula using the product rule. A term differentiating
$\eta$ corresponds uniquely to a shuffle in $W(d\eta,\zeta)$: insert the derivative
index into its first block. Its sign is unchanged. A term differentiating $\zeta$
corresponds to a shuffle in $W(\eta,d\zeta)$; moving the derivative index past the first
$k$ slots introduces $(-1)^k$. Thus
$d(\eta\wedge\zeta)=d\eta\wedge\zeta+(-1)^k\eta\wedge d\zeta$.
All sums and cancellations are finite, and no formula divides by an integer.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Comparison with alternating-bundle sections}

\begin{proposition}[agreement under a retraction]\label{prop:ambient-comparison}
Every intrinsically analytic alternating-valued representative is ambient analytic.
Suppose, moreover, that for the model space $P$ there is a bounded linear retraction
\[
 R\colon\Mult^k(P;F)\longrightarrow\Alt^k(P;F),\qquad Rj=\id.
\]
Then the natural atlas of $\Alt^k(TM;F)$ is analytic, and its analytic sections are
exactly $\Omega^k_{\mathrm{amb}}(M;F)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Postcomposition with $j$ proves the first assertion. Conversely, an ambient analytic
representative $j\eta$ gives an intrinsically analytic one by $\eta=R(j\eta)$.
The formula
$P(u_1,\ldots,u_k)(m)=R\bigl(m\circ(u_1,\ldots,u_k)\bigr)$
is a bounded multilinear lift of the precomposition action on $\Alt^k(P;F)$.
Thus this action, and its composition with the derivatives of chart changes, is analytic.
The resulting analytic bundle atlas makes the preceding comparison chart-independent.
\end{proof}

This hypothesis holds when $k!\ne0$: the normalized antisymmetrizer in the $k$ form
variables is a bounded retraction. It also holds for nonarchimedean, spherically complete
$F$, by \S\ref{sec:theoremA}, and for finite-coordinate $P$ by the basis construction.
These are sufficient conditions; necessity of splitting is not asserted.
Analyticity of a chosen bundle atlas alone does not imply agreement.
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-pullback} exhibits the difference even over a complete field:
in the standard product trivializations on $N$ and $Y$, both pointwise alternating bundles
are analytic product bundles, yet the ambient analytic pullback is not an intrinsic analytic
section on $N$. This observation does not assert analyticity of the atlas induced by
\emph{all} analytic coordinate changes on $N$.

The ambient calculus is therefore available independently of the universal scalar-target
criterion. The obstruction concerns the stronger bundle construction and intrinsic
operator-norm analyticity of its coefficient maps. It neither prevents the definition
of differential forms nor destroys pullback, wedge products, or exterior differentiation
for the ambient definition.

\section{The largest analytic domain}\label{sec:domains}

The analytic cases of Section~\ref{sec:functor-bundles} in degrees with $k!=0$ in $K$ rest on hypotheses on
objects: finite continuous coordinates on a source, or a spherically complete nonarchimedean target. This
suggests restricting the objects on which the alternating-map bifunctor acts. There is, however, an obstruction
to a single best restriction: two objects can each have analytic endomorphism action while the action on
morphisms between them is not analytic.

The bundle problem suggests the same restriction of objects. By Section~\ref{sec:nonanalytic-bundles}, when $k!=0$ in $K$
the analytic construction of alternating-map bundles cannot work for all fibers over all bases.
Section~\ref{sec:families-bundles} restricted the base. Here the base stays arbitrary, and we ask for the largest
class of fiber pairs on which $\Alt^k$ is analytic.

We define analytic domains and record their bundles (Corollary~\ref{cor:domain-bundles}). The targets $F$ for
which $A^k_{E,D;F}$ is analytic for all normed $E,D$ form a canonical core compatible with every analytic domain
(Proposition~\ref{dom:core}). Within any set of objects, every analytic domain extends to a maximal one
(Proposition~\ref{dom:maximal}), and pairs split in the sense of Definition~\ref{dom:split-pair} form a further
analytic domain (Lemma~\ref{dom:split-incoming}). A largest analytic domain need not exist: for every prime $p$
and every $k\ge p$, over $K=\F_p(t)$ with its $t$\nobreakdash-adic absolute value, there is no largest full
analytic domain (Theorem~\ref{dom:no-largest}, with the construction in Appendix~\ref{app:no-largest}).
The section ends with what is known about universal targets (\S\ref{ssec:universal-targets}).

\begin{definition}\label{dom:definition}
A full subcategory $\mathcal D\subseteq\mathsf{Vec}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathsf{Vec}_K$ is an
\emph{analytic domain} for $\Alt^k$ if the restriction of $\Alt^k$ to $\mathcal D$ is analytic on every
hom space. Let $\mathsf T_k(K)$ be the full subcategory of normed spaces $F$ such that
$A^k_{E,D;F}$ is analytic for every pair of normed spaces $E,D$.
\end{definition}

\begin{corollary}[bundles with fibers in an analytic domain]\label{cor:domain-bundles}
Let $\mathcal D$ be an analytic domain for $\Alt^k$, and let $M$ be an analytic manifold over $K$ modeled on an
arbitrary normed space. Consider the category whose objects are pairs $(\mathcal E,\mathcal H)$ of analytic normed
vector bundles over $M$ whose typical fiber pair $(E,F)$ is an object of $\mathcal D$. A morphism
$(\mathcal E,\mathcal H)\to(\mathcal E',\mathcal H')$ is a pair of analytic bundle morphisms
$\varphi\colon\mathcal E'\to\mathcal E$ and $\psi\colon\mathcal H\to\mathcal H'$; since $\mathcal D$ is full, its local
expressions are analytic families of morphisms of $\mathcal D$ from $(E,F)$ to $(E',F')$. On this category,
fiberwise alternating maps define a functor $(\mathcal E,\mathcal H)\mapsto\Alt^k(\mathcal E;\mathcal H)$ with values in
analytic normed vector bundles over $M$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Over a common trivializing cover, the transition maps $g_{ij}$ of $\mathcal E$ and $h_{ij}$ of $\mathcal H$ give an analytic
family $x\mapsto(g_{ji}(x),h_{ij}(x))$ of endomorphisms of $(E,F)$ in $\mathcal D$. Changes of trivialization and local
expressions of morphisms are analytic families in hom spaces of $\mathcal D$ in the same way. The action of $\Alt^k$ on
these hom spaces is analytic, so its composites with all these families are analytic. This is the hypothesis of
Proposition~\ref{prop:bundle-construction}(2), the form of part~(1) that requires regularity only along the families
that occur, and it gives the bundle $\Alt^k(\mathcal E;\mathcal H)$ together with its functoriality.
\end{proof}

Corollary~\ref{cor:analytic-bundles} is the special case of Corollary~\ref{cor:domain-bundles} for two analytic domains. The first is the core
$\mathsf{Vec}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathsf T_k(K)$ of Proposition~\ref{dom:core} below, whose target class contains the
target fibers of the factorial and spherically complete cases. The second consists of the pairs whose source
has a finite algebraic basis with continuous coordinate functionals; it is an analytic domain by
Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-coordinate} and~\eqref{eq:joint-action}. A largest analytic domain, if it existed, would
carry the largest such bundle construction valid over every analytic base. This motivates the question studied next.

\subsection{A core compatible with every analytic domain}

The definition of an analytic domain includes morphisms between distinct objects. Requiring only that each
endomorphism action be analytic is strictly weaker, as Theorem~\ref{dom:no-largest} will show.

\begin{proposition}[The universal-target core]\label{dom:core}
The full subcategory $\mathsf{Vec}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathsf T_k(K)$ is an analytic domain. It is the
largest analytic domain of the form $\mathsf{Vec}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal T$. Moreover, adjoining all
its objects to any analytic domain again gives an analytic domain. The category $\mathsf T_k(K)$ is closed
under bounded linear isomorphisms, bounded linear retracts, and finite products.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Consider a morphism from $(E,F)$ to $(D,G)$, represented by $u\colon D\to E$ and $v\colon F\to G$.
Writing $v_*$ for postcomposition, its action has the two factorizations
\begin{equation}\label{dom:two-factorizations}
\Alt^k(u,v)
 =v_*\circ A^k_{D,E;F}(u)
 =A^k_{D,E;G}(u)\circ v_*.
\end{equation}
Operator composition is bounded bilinear. The first expression is therefore jointly analytic if
$F\in\mathsf T_k(K)$, and the second is jointly analytic if $G\in\mathsf T_k(K)$. Thus a universally
analytic target at either endpoint suffices. This proves both analyticity of the core and its compatibility
with every analytic domain. Conversely, analyticity on a product with unrestricted first variable, restricted
to $v=\id_F$, is exactly the defining property of $\mathsf T_k(K)$.

Suppose $i\colon F\to G$ and $r\colon G\to F$ are bounded linear maps with $r i=\id_F$. Then
\[
A^k_{E,D;F}(u)=r_*\circ A^k_{E,D;G}(u)\circ i_*.
\]
Consequently the property passes from $G$ to its retract $F$, and in particular is invariant under bounded
linear isomorphism. For a finite product of targets, alternating maps identify boundedly with the product
of the corresponding alternating-map spaces; under this identification, pullback acts componentwise.
This proves closure under finite products.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Compatibility and maximal domains]\label{dom:maximal}
Fix a set of objects in the ambient product category. Form a graph whose vertices are those objects
with analytic endomorphism action, joining two distinct vertices when both directed morphism-space
actions are analytic. Full analytic domains are exactly the cliques in this graph. Every analytic
domain extends to one maximal under inclusion, and every maximal domain contains every object of the
universal-target core that belongs to the chosen set.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The clique condition states exactly that every hom-space action in the full subcategory is analytic.
The union of a chain of cliques is again a clique: any two of its vertices occur together in one member
of the chain. Zorn's lemma gives a maximal extension. Proposition~\ref{dom:core} permits adjoining every
core object, so maximality forces each such object to be present.
\end{proof}

This description distinguishes existence of maximal restrictions from existence of a largest one.
The counterexample below exhibits two vertices that are not joined. Thus no largest clique exists in any
chosen ambient set containing both constructed objects.
The set-of-objects qualification makes the use of Zorn's lemma explicit; no choice principle for a proper
class of objects is being asserted.

The core is canonical, but its definition is a regularity condition rather than an intrinsic classification
of targets. Main Theorem~(\ref{main:spherical}) supplies many of its objects. A different sufficient condition
depends on the source and target together.

\begin{definition}\label{dom:split-pair}
A pair $(E,F)$ is \emph{split in degree $k$} if the isometric inclusion
\[
j_{E,F}\colon\Alt^k(E;F)\longrightarrow\Mult^k(E;F)
\]
has a bounded linear retraction.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{dom:split-incoming}
Every morphism-space action whose destination is a split pair is analytic. In particular, the full
subcategory of split pairs is an analytic domain.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $(E,F)$ be split, with retraction $R$, and let the other object be $(D,G)$. For $u\colon E\to D$
and $v\colon G\to F$, the action is
\[
m\longmapsto R\bigl(v\circ m\circ(u,\ldots,u)\bigr).
\]
Before applying $R$, replace the $k$ copies of $u$ by independent variables. This gives a bounded
$(k+1)$\nobreakdash-linear lift of the joint action, with values in
$\cL(\Alt^k(D;G),\Mult^k(E;F))$. Postcomposition with $R$ gives a lift with the required alternating
codomain. No compatibility between the retractions chosen for different objects is needed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Two individually analytic objects with nonanalytic cross-action}

\begin{theorem}[No largest full analytic domain]\label{dom:no-largest}
Let $p$ be prime, let $k\ge p$, and equip $K=\F_p(t)$ with its $t$\nobreakdash-adic absolute value.
There is no largest full analytic domain for
\[
\Alt^k\colon\mathsf{Vec}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathsf{Vec}_K\longrightarrow\mathsf{Vec}_K.
\]
The assertion remains true if the domains must be closed under isomorphisms, and if their objects are
restricted to pairs of finite-dimensional, nonarchimedean normed $K$\nobreakdash-spaces. In the latter case,
isomorphism closure is understood inside that restricted ambient category.

More precisely, there are objects $X_k=(E_k,G)$ and $Y_k=(D_k,G)$ whose singleton full subcategories
are analytic, such that $Y_k$ is split and the action on morphisms from $Y_k$ to $X_k$ is not analytic.
\end{theorem}

Here finite-dimensional means finite \emph{algebraic} dimension over the incomplete field $K$. These spaces
need not admit continuous coordinates over $K$. The theorem does not give a counterexample over a complete
field, or a counterexample with Banach objects.

The proof of Theorem~\ref{dom:no-largest} is given in Appendix~\ref{app:no-largest}. We outline it and fix the
objects used in the remarks below. Let $L=\F_p((t))$ be the completion of $K$, and let $A=L[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^p)$
carry the maximum norm in the basis $e_i=\epsilon^i$ $(0\le i<p)$. Choose $a=\sum_ia_ie_i$ with
$a_0,\ldots,a_{p-1}\in L$ such that these scalars and finitely many auxiliary scalars $\tau_w\in L$ are
algebraically independent over $K$. Then $E=K^p+Ka\subseteq A$ is a $K$\nobreakdash-space of dimension $p+1$ with
completion $A$. By Lemma~\ref{dom:rigid} it is rigid: its bounded endomorphisms are the scalars in $K$, and it has
no nonzero bounded linear functional. Let $D\subseteq A$ be spanned by $K^p$, the vectors $\epsilon^ia$ and $a^2$,
and the generators $\tau_ww$, where $w$ runs over the vectors $e_i$ and $e_i+e_j$ with $i<j$. Let $G\subseteq L$ be
spanned by the determinants, in the basis $(e_i)$, of $p$\nobreakdash-tuples from $D$; the completions of $D$ and $G$
are $A$ and $L$. By rigidity, the self-action of $X=(E,G)$ is $(s,v)\mapsto s^pv_*$, so its singleton is analytic.
The generators $\tau_ww$ make every bounded $p$\nobreakdash-linear map $D^p\to G$ alternating, so $Y=(D,G)$ is split
(Lemma~\ref{dom:all-alt}) and its singleton is analytic by Lemma~\ref{dom:split-incoming}.

Multiplication by $x\in E$ in $A$ gives a bounded linear family $E\to\cL(E,D)$, $x\mapsto M_x$, with
$\det M_x=x_0^p$, where $x_0$ is the $e_0$\nobreakdash-coefficient of $x$. Identify $\Alt^p(E;G)$ with $C\det$, where
$C=\{\lambda\in G:a_i\lambda\in G\text{ for every }i\}$, as in~\eqref{dom:coefficient-space}. Through this
identification, the cross-action from $Y$ to $X$ along $x\mapsto M_x$, with target morphism $\id_G$ and evaluated
at $\det$, is the coordinate map $x\mapsto x_0^p$ of~\eqref{dom:bad-coordinate}. This map has no bounded
$p$\nobreakdash-linear lift, because a coefficient is missing: a lift would place in $C$ an element whose specialization
at $\tau_w=0$ has a nonzero homogeneous component of degree one in the $a_i$, and Lemma~\ref{dom:degree-gap} shows
that the specialized coefficient space has no such component. Analyticity at zero would produce a lift by the
homogeneous coefficient argument of Proposition~\ref{prop:lift}, so the cross-action is not analytic.

For $k=p+r$ we pad by direct sums: $E_k=E\oplus K^r$ and $D_k=D\oplus K^r$, the bounded affine family
$u(x)=M_x\oplus I_r$ in $\cL(E_k,D_k)$, and $\det_k$ in place of $\det$. This gives $X_k=(E_k,G)$ and
$Y_k=(D_k,G)$ with the same properties. Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-reflection} does not apply to the family $u$: its parameter space $E$ has finite
algebraic dimension, but no nonzero bounded linear functional and hence no continuous coordinates.

\begin{remark}\label{dom:limits}
The theorem also shows that $X_k$ cannot be adjoined to the entire split-pair category, since that category
contains $Y_k$. It does not establish that the split-pair category has no other strict analytic extension.
Nor does it resolve the largest-domain question for complete fields or Banach pairs. Completing this
particular construction removes the obstruction: the sources become finite-dimensional $L$\nobreakdash-spaces,
the target becomes $L$, and the coordinate polynomial acquires a bounded multilinear lift.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[finite algebraic dimension does not suffice for bundles]\label{dom:finite-algebraic-base}
The same objects show that the coordinate hypothesis in Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-bundles} cannot be weakened
to finite algebraic dimension over an incomplete field. Take $M=E$, of dimension $p+1$ over $K=\F_p(t)$, as an
analytic manifold with one chart; by Lemma~\ref{dom:rigid} it has no nonzero bounded linear functional. Let the
fiber be $H=D_k\times E_k$ with two global trivializations differing by the shear
$g(x)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\id&u(x)\\0&\id\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, where $u(x)=M_x\oplus I_r$ is the bounded affine
family of Appendix~\ref{dom:section}, and take the trivial target bundle with fiber $G$. The transitions $g(x)$ and $g(x)^{-1}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\id&-u(x)\\0&\id\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$ are affine
analytic. By~\eqref{fam:eq:shear-retract}, compressing the induced pullback recovers $A^k_{E_k,D_k;G}(u(x))$, and evaluation
at $\det_k$ followed by coefficient extraction gives the map~\eqref{dom:bad-coordinate}, which is not analytic at zero.
Hence the alternating-map bundle is not analytic, although the base and all fibers have finite algebraic dimension.
Over a complete field this cannot happen, by Lemma~\ref{lem:findim}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Universal targets}\label{ssec:universal-targets}

By Proposition~\ref{dom:core}, the class $\mathsf T_k(K)$ of Definition~\ref{dom:definition} determines a canonical
analytic domain, $\mathsf{Vec}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathsf T_k(K)$, whose objects can be adjoined to every other
analytic domain. The search for large analytic domains therefore leads to the question of which targets are
universal. We collect what is known.

If $k!\ne0$ in $K$, then $\mathsf T_k(K)$ contains every normed space (Proposition~\ref{prop:factorial-positive}).
If $K$ has positive characteristic, Main Theorem~(\ref{main:spherical}) places every nonarchimedean, spherically
complete space in $\mathsf T_k(K)$ for every $k$. Since $\mathsf T_k(K)$ is closed under bounded linear isomorphisms,
retracts and finite products (Proposition~\ref{dom:core}), it then contains every space that admits an equivalent
nonarchimedean, spherically complete norm, and every bounded linear retract of such a space. If $K$ has
characteristic $p>0$ and $k\ge p$, then $\mathsf T_k(K)$ does not contain the Banach target of
Theorem~\ref{thm:main}, which admits no equivalent nonarchimedean norm.

Remark~\ref{rem:finite-target-reduction} and Proposition~\ref{prop:discrete} below treat finite-dimensional targets
and targets over discretely valued fields. In terms of universal targets, they give the following. Over a complete
field, a nonzero finite-dimensional space lies in $\mathsf T_k(K)$ exactly when the scalar target $K$ does.
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-classification} now settles this membership: over a complete nonarchimedean $K$, it is
equivalent to $k!\ne0$ in $K$ or spherical completeness of $K$. Over a
nonarchimedean field with $\abs{K^\times}=r^\Z$ for some $r\in(0,1)$, complete or not, every Banach space that admits
an equivalent nonarchimedean norm lies in $\mathsf T_k(K)$ for every $k$. Remark~\ref{rem:nonspherical-analytic-target}
in Appendix~\ref{app:further} shows that spherical completeness of the given norm is not necessary for membership.
Its target still admits an equivalent spherically complete norm, so the example does not decide whether such a norm
is necessary. Section~\ref{sec:open} asks for an intrinsic characterization of $\mathsf T_k(K)$.

\begin{proposition}[universal targets over a nonspherically complete field]\label{prop:universal-zero-dual}
Let $K$ be complete, nontrivially normed and not spherically complete, and suppose $k! = 0$ in $K$.
Then every $F\in\mathsf T_k(K)$ has zero continuous dual. More precisely, the Banach spaces $E,E'$ of
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-counter} make $A^k_{E,E';F}$ analytic at no point for every normed target $F$
with a nonzero continuous linear functional.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Choose $\lambda\in\cL(F,K)$ and $v\in F$ with $\lambda(v)=1$. The maps $a\mapsto va$ and
$m\mapsto\lambda\circ m$ on alternating-map spaces are bounded linear. For every $u\in\cL(E,E')$,
\[
A^k_{E,E';K}(u)(a)=\lambda\circ A^k_{E,E';F}(u)(va).
\]
Thus a fixed bounded linear compression sends $A^k_{E,E';F}(u)$ to $A^k_{E,E';K}(u)$.
Analyticity of the former at any point would contradict Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-counter}.
\end{proof}
In particular no nonzero finite-dimensional space, dual space or polar space is universal in this regime.
For a nonzero dual space, evaluation at a suitable vector is a nonzero bounded functional; for a polar
space, the defining family of bounded functionals separates points. The proposition supplies a necessary
condition only: zero continuous dual has not been shown sufficient for universality.

\begin{remark}[finite-dimensional targets]\label{rem:finite-target-reduction}
Let $K$ be a complete nontrivially normed field, let $E,E'$ be normed $K$\nobreakdash-spaces, and let $F$ be finite-dimensional.
If $F\ne0$, then, at every $f_0\in\cL(E,E')$,
\[
A^k_{E,E';F}\text{ is analytic at }f_0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
A^k_{E,E';K}\text{ is analytic at }f_0.
\]
If $F=0$, precomposition is constant and hence analytic.

Indeed, choose a basis $v_1,\dots,v_d$ of $F$ with coordinate functionals
$\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_d$. These functionals are continuous because $K$ is complete. For a vector $v$ and a scalar-valued form $a$, write $va$ for the form $x\mapsto a(x)v$. Writing
$A_F:=A^k_{E,E';F}$ and $A_K:=A^k_{E,E';K}$, we have
\[
A_F(f)(m)=\sum_{a=1}^d v_a\,A_K(f)(\lambda_a\circ m).
\]
The right-hand side is a fixed continuous linear function of the operator $A_K(f)$, so analyticity
of $A_K$ implies that of $A_F$. Conversely, choose $v\in F$ and $\lambda\in\cL(F,K)$ with
$\lambda(v)=1$. For $a\in\Alt^k(E';K)$,
\[
A_K(f)(a)=\lambda\circ A_F(f)(v a).
\]
This expresses $A_K$ as a fixed continuous linear function of $A_F$ and proves the converse.
Together with Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-classification}, this proves that a nonzero finite-dimensional target
is universal over complete nonarchimedean $K$ exactly when $k!\ne0$ or $K$ is spherically complete.
In particular, finite-dimensionality of the target alone does not ensure analyticity.

If $K$ is nonarchimedean and spherically complete, there is a positive conclusion for every
finite-dimensional $F$, with any norm. The coordinate norm
$\norm y_\infty:=\max_a\abs{\lambda_a(y)}$ is equivalent to the given norm. With this norm, $F$ is
spherically complete: a closed ball in $K^d$ is a product of coordinate balls, and spherical completeness
of $K$ gives a point in each coordinate intersection. The Main Theorem therefore applies. Returning to the
original norm preserves analyticity, since it changes the norms on the alternating-map and operator
spaces only by fixed comparison factors.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[targets over discretely valued bases]\label{prop:discrete}
Let $K$ be a nonarchimedean normed field with $\abs{K^\times}=r^\Z$ for some $r\in(0,1)$, complete or
not; for instance $K=\F_q((t))$, or $K=\kappa((t))$ for an arbitrary field $\kappa$. Let $E,E'$ be normed $K$\nobreakdash-spaces and $F$ a $K$\nobreakdash-Banach space that
admits an equivalent nonarchimedean norm. Then $A^k_{E,E';F}$ is analytic at every point.
\end{proposition}
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{app:further}.

Thus, over a discretely valued base, every Banach counterexample must have a target admitting no equivalent
nonarchimedean norm. Proposition~\ref{prop:nonultra} establishes this property for the target of
Theorem~\ref{thm:main} over every base.

\section{Open problems}\label{sec:open}\label{sec:complements}

The distinction between restrictions on objects and restrictions on analytic families leaves several
different classification problems. They are substantive only in characteristic $p>0$ and degree $k\ge p$;
when $k!\ne0$, the alternating-map bifunctor is already analytic on the whole product category. For
$C^\infty$ smoothness, no restriction is needed in any characteristic.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Intrinsic characterization of universal targets.}
Determine $\mathsf T_k(K)$ in terms of the topological linear structure of its objects, rather than by
restating analyticity or the multilinear lift criterion. In particular, characterize its Banach objects
and the targets that belong to $\mathsf T_k(K)$ in every finite degree.
By \S\ref{ssec:universal-targets}, an equivalent nonarchimedean, spherically complete norm is sufficient,
and spherical completeness of the given norm is not necessary; the example there does not decide whether
such an equivalent norm is necessary. Over complete nonspherically complete $K$, Proposition~\ref{prop:universal-zero-dual}
adds the necessary condition $F^*=0$ when $k! = 0$. Is that condition sufficient within any substantial
class of Banach targets, or what further structure is required?

\item \emph{Analytic domains over complete fields and among Banach pairs.}
For a prescribed complete field $K$, does there exist a largest full analytic domain, closed under
isomorphisms, in $\mathsf{Vec}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathsf{Vec}_K$? Ask the same question in
$\mathsf{Ban}_K^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathsf{Ban}_K$, also when $K$ itself is incomplete.
Theorem~\ref{dom:no-largest} answers the unrestricted existence question negatively over $\F_p(t)$,
using finite-dimensional but incomplete spaces. Its construction does not decide either of these
restricted questions. Where no largest domain exists, seek structural criteria for compatibility between
objects and descriptions of full domains maximal under inclusion.

\item \emph{Extensions of the split-pair category.}
Which objects can be adjoined to the full category of pairs for which
$\Alt^k(E;F)\hookrightarrow\Mult^k(E;F)$ has a bounded linear retraction?
The universal-target core can always be adjoined, by Proposition~\ref{dom:core}.
The objects $X_k$ of Theorem~\ref{dom:no-largest}, despite having analytic self-action, cannot be adjoined.
A useful answer should give sufficient and necessary conditions on the spaces themselves, rather than
merely requiring analyticity of all the cross-actions.

\item \emph{Parameter spaces for analytic families.}
Characterize normed parameter spaces $P$ for which every operator-valued analytic family with parameters
in an open subset of $P$ induces an analytic family on alternating-map spaces. The hypotheses on the
target fibers should be recorded explicitly: completeness and nonarchimedean norms enter some of the
positive results. General reflection of analyticity through closed linear embeddings is a sufficient
condition, but it is stronger than the property needed for this particular finite-degree construction.
The ordinary $\ell^1$ parameter theorem makes this distinction concrete.
The answer depends on the admitted target fibers: by Corollaries~\ref{fam:cor:c0-families}
and~\ref{lau:cor:diagonal-bundle}, the space $c_0(\N,\F_q((u)))$ qualifies for complete nonarchimedean
final targets but not for all complete targets.

\item \emph{Countable index.}
Let $K$ be complete, not spherically complete, of characteristic $p\ge3$, and let $k\ge p$.
Is there a complete nonarchimedean $F$ such that $A^k_{E,E;F}$ is not analytic for
$E=\linf(\N,K)$? For $p=2$, the answer is yes: degree two is the theorem of~\cite{M}, and the higher
degrees follow by the wedging argument of~\cite[Remark~18]{M}, formalized under hypothesis (K2) of that
paper (Remark~\ref{rem:K2}). Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-counter} now gives a counterexample with $F=K$ and
source spaces that are closed subspaces of countable bounded-product spaces. It does not prescribe either
source space to be $\linf(\N,K)$, so the fixed-source question here remains open.
Remark~\ref{rem:R} in Appendix~\ref{app:further} records a proposed determinant-array construction with a larger
prescribed bounded-product source; that specific construction also remains unproved.

\item \emph{Ambient and intrinsic scalar forms on a fixed model space.}
Let $K$ be complete, not spherically complete, of characteristic $p$, and let $k\ge p$.
Which model spaces $P$ make the naturally induced scalar alternating cotangent atlas analytic for every
analytic manifold modeled on $P$? Countable type is sufficient (Proposition~\ref{prop:scalar-countable});
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-tangent} excludes unrestricted Banach models. If all analytic coordinate changes on
$P$ induce analytic alternating transitions, must ambient analytic forms on open subsets of $P$ be
intrinsically analytic? Proposition~\ref{prop:ambient-comparison} proves this under a bounded retraction.
The chosen product atlases in Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-pullback} do not settle the stronger question with
compatibility required for every chart of the maximal analytic atlas.

\item \emph{Densely valued spherically complete bases.}
Let $K$ be spherically complete, densely valued and of characteristic $p$, for example
$\F_p((t^{\mathbb Q}))$, and let $k\ge p$. Is $A^k_{E,E';F}$ analytic for all Banach $E,E'$ and every
complete nonarchimedean $F$? Main Theorem~(\ref{main:spherical}) gives a positive answer for spherically
complete $F$. The counterexample of Theorem~\ref{thm:main} has a target with no equivalent nonarchimedean
norm and therefore does not answer this question.
\end{enumerate}

\appendix

\section{Proofs for the lift criterion and finite coordinates}\label{app:lift}

This appendix proves the lift criterion of Section~\ref{sec:preliminaries} (Proposition~\ref{prop:lift}) from the one-variable uniqueness of power-series coefficients (Lemma~\ref{lem:unique}), so the argument uses no polarization formula, no division by $k!$ and no completeness assumption. It then gives the explicit determinant lifts of Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-coordinate} from Section~\ref{sec:functor-bundles}, for spaces with a finite algebraic basis whose coordinate functionals are continuous; these lifts are finite sums and are valid in every characteristic.

\begin{lemma}[one-variable uniqueness]\label{lem:unique}
Let $K$ be nontrivially normed and $Z$ a normed $K$\nobreakdash-space, not necessarily complete. Let $z_0,z_1,\dots\in Z$, $\rho>0$
and $M\ge0$ with $\norm{z_n}\rho^n\le M$ for all $n$. If $\sum_nt^nz_n$ converges to $0$ for every $t\in K$ with
$\abs t<\rho$, then $z_n=0$ for all $n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Otherwise let $N$ be least with $z_N\neq0$. For $0<\abs t<\rho/2$ the series $\sum_{n>N}t^{n-N}z_n$ converges, being
$t^{-N}$ times the convergent tail $\sum_{n>N}t^nz_n$, and $t^N\bigl(z_N+\sum_{n>N}t^{n-N}z_n\bigr)=0$. Hence
\[
\norm{z_N}=\Bigl\lVert\sum_{n>N}t^{n-N}z_n\Bigr\rVert\le\sum_{n>N}M\rho^{-n}\abs t^{n-N}
=M\rho^{-N}\frac{\abs t/\rho}{1-\abs t/\rho},
\]
where the norm of a convergent series is bounded by the sum of the norms by continuity of the norm on partial sums.
Since $K$ is nontrivially normed, there are $t\ne0$ with $\abs t$ arbitrarily small, so $\norm{z_N}=0$, a
contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:lift}]
(1) For $y\in\cL(E,E')$, multilinearity gives
\[
A(f_0+y)=P(f_0+y,\dots,f_0+y)=\sum_{r=0}^kq_r(y,\dots,y),
\]
where $q_r(y_1,\dots,y_r)$ is the sum, over the $r$\nobreakdash-element subsets $S\subseteq\{1,\dots,k\}$, of $P$ evaluated with
$y_1,\dots,y_r$ in the slots of $S$ in increasing order and $f_0$ in the other slots. Each $q_r$ is
bounded $r$\nobreakdash-linear, with $\norm{q_r}\le\binom kr\norm P\norm{f_0}^{k-r}$, so $(q_r)_{r\le k}$ is a power series of
infinite radius for $A$ at $f_0$.

(2) We compare the given power series along each line with the polynomial obtained by expanding precomposition.
The expansion is taken in the larger space of all multilinear maps, where each coefficient is visibly defined.
Let $\incl\colon\Alt^k(E;F)\hookrightarrow\Mult^k(E;F)$ be the isometric inclusion, and put
\[
Z:=\cL\bigl(\Alt^k(E';F),\Mult^k(E;F)\bigr),\qquad \mathcal I(T):=\incl\circ T .
\]
Then $\mathcal I$ is a linear isometry into $Z$, hence injective.
Fix $h\in\cL(E,E')$, $h\neq0$. For $m\in\Alt^k(E';F)$, $x\in E^k$ and $t\in K$, multilinearity of $m$ gives the
\emph{ambient polynomial identity}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ambient}
\mathcal I\bigl(A(f_0+th)\bigr)=\sum_{r=0}^kt^rC_r(h),\qquad C_r(h)(m)(x):=\sum_{\abs S=r}m(u^S_1,\dots,u^S_k),
\end{equation}
where $u^S_i=hx_i$ for $i\in S$ and $u^S_i=f_0x_i$ for $i\notin S$. Here $C_r(h)\in Z$ is bounded, with
$\norm{C_r(h)}\le\binom kr\norm h^r\norm{f_0}^{k-r}$, and $C_k(h)=\mathcal I(A(h))$. (For $r<k$ we do not need to know whether
$C_r(h)(m)$ is alternating; this is why~\eqref{eq:ambient} is written in $Z$.) Let $R$ and $M':=\sup_n\norm{p_n}R^n$ be
as in the definition of analyticity, and put $\rho:=R/\norm h$. For $\abs t<\rho$ we have $\norm{th}<R$, so
$A(f_0+th)=\sum_nt^np_n(h,\dots,h)$, and applying $\mathcal I$ and~\eqref{eq:ambient} gives $\sum_nt^nz_n=0$ in $Z$, with
$z_n:=\mathcal I(p_n(h,\dots,h))-C_n(h)$ and $C_n(h):=0$ for $n>k$. Since
$\norm{z_n}\rho^n\le M'+\max_{r\le k}\norm{C_r(h)}\rho^r$, Lemma~\ref{lem:unique} gives $z_k=0$, that is,
$\mathcal I(p_k(h,\dots,h))=\mathcal I(A(h))$, hence $p_k(h,\dots,h)=A(h)$. For $h=0$ both sides vanish since $k\ge1$.

(3) follows from (1) and (2), and from \texttt{ContDiffAt.analyticAt} for the last clause.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-coordinate}]
Let $H$ denote the space with a finite coordinate basis, write that basis as $e_1,\dots,e_n$, and let
$\varepsilon_1,\dots,\varepsilon_n\in\cL_K(H,K)$ be its coordinate functionals. For an increasing $k$\nobreakdash-tuple
$s=(s_1<\dots<s_k)$ in $\{1,\dots,n\}$, put
\[
c_s:=\prod_{a=1}^k\norm{\varepsilon_{s_a}}\norm{e_{s_a}},
\qquad C_H:=k!\sum_{s_1<\dots<s_k}c_s.
\]
Here the factor $k!$ in $C_H$ is the positive real integer counting the terms in a determinant, not its image in
$K$. In particular, $C_H\ge0$ even when $k!=0$ in $K$.

Suppose first that $H=E$. For $f_1,\dots,f_k\in\cL_K(E,E')$, $m\in\Alt^k_K(E';F)$ and $x\in E^k$, define
\[
P(f_1,\dots,f_k)(m)(x_1,\dots,x_k)
:=\sum_{s_1<\dots<s_k}
\det\bigl(\varepsilon_{s_a}(x_b)\bigr)_{a,b}\,
 m(f_1e_{s_1},\dots,f_ke_{s_k}).
\]
The determinant makes this expression alternating and multilinear in $x$. It is linear in $m$ and multilinear in
$f_1,\dots,f_k$. Expanding the determinant over permutations gives
\[
\left|\det\bigl(\varepsilon_{s_a}(x_b)\bigr)_{a,b}\right|
\le k!\prod_a\norm{\varepsilon_{s_a}}\prod_b\norm{x_b}.
\]
Together with the operator bound for $m$, the ordinary triangle inequality therefore yields
\[
\norm{P(f_1,\dots,f_k)(m)(x)}
\le C_E\,\norm m\prod_a\norm{f_a}\prod_b\norm{x_b}.
\]
Thus the displayed formula defines a bounded $k$\nobreakdash-linear map into
$\cL_K(\Alt^k_K(E';F),\Alt^k_K(E;F))$, with $\norm P\le C_E$. On the diagonal, the finite exterior-basis expansion
of $x_1\wedge\dots\wedge x_k$ gives
\[
m(fx_1,\dots,fx_k)
=\sum_{s_1<\dots<s_k}
\det\bigl(\varepsilon_{s_a}(x_b)\bigr)_{a,b}\,
 m(fe_{s_1},\dots,fe_{s_k}),
\]
so $P(f,\dots,f)=A(f)$.

Now suppose that $H=E'$. Define instead
\[
P(f_1,\dots,f_k)(m)(x_1,\dots,x_k)
:=\sum_{s_1<\dots<s_k}
\det\bigl(\varepsilon_{s_a}(f_ax_b)\bigr)_{a,b}\,
 m(e_{s_1},\dots,e_{s_k}).
\]
Each row of the determinant is linear in its corresponding operator $f_a$, and its columns make the result
alternating and multilinear in $x$. The expression is again linear in $m$. The determinant estimate now reads
\[
\left|\det\bigl(\varepsilon_{s_a}(f_ax_b)\bigr)_{a,b}\right|
\le k!\prod_a\bigl(\norm{\varepsilon_{s_a}}\norm{f_a}\bigr)\prod_b\norm{x_b}.
\]
It follows that the same operator bound holds with $C_{E'}$ in place of $C_E$. Expanding
$fx_1\wedge\dots\wedge fx_k$ in the exterior basis of $E'$ proves the diagonal identity.

Both formulas are finite sums. Strong alternation follows from equal columns in a determinant, so the proof works
in every characteristic and involves no division by $k!$. Proposition~\ref{prop:lift} gives continuous
polynomiality and analyticity. If $k>n$, the sums are empty and the relevant alternating-map space is zero; the
same argument gives the zero lift. Degree zero, excluded from the lift criterion's statement, is immediate because
$A$ is constant.
\end{proof}

\section{Spherically complete targets}\label{app:spherical}

This appendix proves Main Theorem~(\ref{main:spherical}), following the strategy of \S\ref{sec:theoremA}.
Throughout, $K$ is a nonarchimedean nontrivially normed field, $E$ and $E'$ are normed $K$\nobreakdash-spaces,
and $F$ is a nonarchimedean, spherically complete normed $K$\nobreakdash-space; for $k\ge1$ we keep the notation
$W=\Alt^k(E;F)$ and $M=\Mult^k(E;F)$.
We prove an extension lemma with simultaneous bounds (Lemma~\ref{lem:compatible-extension}), deduce
Proposition~\ref{prop:alt-spherical} from it, and apply it once more to construct a contraction
$\rho\colon M\to W$ that fixes every alternating map, which gives the lift described in \S\ref{sec:theoremA}.

\subsection{Extension with simultaneous bounds}
The following form of the nonarchimedean extension argument keeps a prospective extension in several operator
balls at once. We allow a seminorm on the domain, since the free space used in the next step may have
nonzero vectors of seminorm zero.

\begin{lemma}[compatible extension]\label{lem:compatible-extension}
Let $U$ be a nonarchimedean seminormed $K$\nobreakdash-space and let $G$ be a nonarchimedean, spherically complete
normed $K$\nobreakdash-space. Suppose a nonempty family $(T_\alpha,r_\alpha)$, with
$T_\alpha\in\cL(U,G)$ and $r_\alpha\ge0$, satisfies
\[
\norm{T_\alpha-T_\beta}\le\max(r_\alpha,r_\beta)
\qquad\text{for all }\alpha,\beta.
\]
Let $D\subseteq U$ be a linear subspace and let $T_0\colon D\to G$ be linear with
\[
\norm{T_0d-T_\alpha d}\le r_\alpha\norm d
\qquad(d\in D,\ \text{all }\alpha).
\]
Then $T_0$ extends to a bounded linear map $T\colon U\to G$ such that
$\norm{T-T_\alpha}\le r_\alpha$ for every $\alpha$. In particular, every contraction from a subspace of $U$
to $G$ extends to a contraction on $U$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to extend across one vector $x\notin D$ while preserving every bound. The required value $Tx$
must belong to the balls
\begin{equation}\label{eq:extension-balls}
\overline B\bigl(T_\alpha(d+x)-T_0d,\ r_\alpha\norm{d+x}\bigr)
\qquad(\alpha,\ d\in D).
\end{equation}
We check that these balls meet pairwise. Write $c_{\alpha,d}=T_\alpha(d+x)-T_0d$, and suppose
$r_\alpha\le r_\beta$. Then
\[
c_{\alpha,d}-c_{\beta,e}
=T_\alpha(d-e)-T_0(d-e)+(T_\alpha-T_\beta)(e+x).
\]
Since $d-e\in D$ and $\norm{d-e}\le\max(\norm{d+x},\norm{e+x})$, the hypotheses give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\norm{c_{\alpha,d}-c_{\beta,e}}
&\le\max\bigl(r_\alpha\norm{d-e},r_\beta\norm{e+x}\bigr)\\
&\le\max\bigl(r_\alpha\norm{d+x},r_\beta\norm{e+x}\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]
This is exactly the condition for the two balls to meet. The other ordering of the radii is symmetric.

By spherical completeness, choose $c$ in all the balls~\eqref{eq:extension-balls}. Define
\[
T(d+\lambda x)=T_0d+\lambda c.
\]
The decomposition is unique because $x\notin D$. For $\lambda\ne0$, apply the ball condition with
$\lambda^{-1}d$ in place of $d$ and multiply by $\abs\lambda$; this gives
$\norm{T(d+\lambda x)-T_\alpha(d+\lambda x)}\le r_\alpha\norm{d+\lambda x}$.
For $\lambda=0$ the original bound applies.

Partially order the extensions satisfying all these inequalities by inclusion of their domains.
Unions of chains remain linear and preserve the bounds, so Zorn's lemma gives a maximal extension.
The one-vector argument forces its domain to be all of $U$. Fixing any one index $\alpha$ gives
$\norm{Tu}\le\max(\norm{T_\alpha},r_\alpha)\norm u$, so the extension is bounded.
For the last assertion, take the single pair $(T_\alpha,r_\alpha)=(0,1)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Spherical completeness of alternating-map spaces}
Ordinary completeness alone would not let us apply the extension lemma with target $W$.
We prove the required stronger property by presenting alternating maps as linear maps on a free space
subject to linear relations. The nonarchimedean seminorm on that free space is chosen so that this presentation
preserves the operator norm, even when $E$ itself is not nonarchimedean.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:alt-spherical}]
For $k=0$, the space identifies isometrically with $F$. Suppose $k\ge1$. Let $U$ be the free
$K$\nobreakdash-vector space on the set $E^k$, with generators $[x]$ and seminorm
\[
\Bigl\lVert\sum_x c_x[x]\Bigr\rVert_U
=\max_x\abs{c_x}\prod_{i=1}^k\norm{x_i}.
\]
The maximum is over the finite support, with empty maximum zero. This seminorm is nonarchimedean because
the absolute value on $K$ is nonarchimedean. Let $R\subseteq U$ be the linear span of the relations expressing
additivity and scalar homogeneity in each slot, together with $[x]$ whenever two coordinates of $x$ coincide.

An alternating map $a\in W$ defines a linear map vanishing on $R$ by
\[
\widehat a\Bigl(\sum_xc_x[x]\Bigr)=\sum_xc_xa(x).
\]
The nonarchimedean inequality in $F$ gives
\[
\Bigl\lVert\widehat a\Bigl(\sum_xc_x[x]\Bigr)\Bigr\rVert
\le\max_x\abs{c_x}\norm{a(x)}
\le\norm a\Bigl\lVert\sum_xc_x[x]\Bigr\rVert_U.
\]
Conversely, a bounded linear map $T\colon U\to F$ vanishing on $R$ defines an alternating map
$a(x)=T([x])$ with $\norm{a(x)}\le\norm T\prod_i\norm{x_i}$. Thus these two constructions are inverse
linear isometries. In particular, the equal-input relations impose strong alternation in every characteristic.

Take a nonempty family of pairwise-meeting closed balls in $W$, with centres $a_\alpha$ and radii $r_\alpha$.
Their corresponding linear maps satisfy
$\norm{\widehat a_\alpha-\widehat a_\beta}\le\max(r_\alpha,r_\beta)$ and vanish on $R$.
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:compatible-extension} with $D=R$, $T_0=0$ and $G=F$.
It produces a bounded linear map $T\colon U\to F$ that vanishes on $R$ and satisfies
$\norm{T-\widehat a_\alpha}\le r_\alpha$ for every $\alpha$. The associated alternating map belongs
to every original ball. This proves spherical completeness; no closure assumption on $R$ is needed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The retraction and the analytic lift}
We now apply the extension lemma a second time, using the alternating-map space itself as the target.
This produces the projection needed to turn ordinary precomposition into a polynomial with alternating values.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Main Theorem~(\ref{main:spherical})]
In degree zero, $\Alt^0(-;F)$ is naturally $F$ and the bifunctor acts by the pushforward morphism, so the conclusion holds. Assume $k\ge1$ and retain $W=\Alt^k(E;F)$ and
$M=\Mult^k(E;F)$. Their operator norms are nonarchimedean, and $W$ is spherically complete by
Proposition~\ref{prop:alt-spherical}. The inclusion $j\colon W\to M$ is a linear isometry.
Its inverse on $j(W)$ is therefore a contraction. Lemma~\ref{lem:compatible-extension} extends this inverse to
a contraction $\rho\colon M\to W$ with $\rho j=\id_W$.

For $f_1,\dots,f_k\in\cL(E,E')$ and $m\in\Alt^k(E';F)$, define
\[
P(f_1,\dots,f_k)(m)=\rho\bigl(m\circ(f_1,\dots,f_k)\bigr).
\]
This is linear in $m$ and multilinear in the $f_i$. The estimate
\[
\norm{P(f_1,\dots,f_k)(m)}\le\norm m\prod_{i=1}^k\norm{f_i}
\]
shows that $P$ is a bounded $k$\nobreakdash-linear map into $\cL(\Alt^k(E';F),\Alt^k(E;F))$, with
$\norm P\le1$. On the diagonal, $m\circ(f,\dots,f)$ is already alternating, so
$P(f,\dots,f)=A(f)$.

For completeness, expand at an arbitrary $f_0\in\cL(E,E')$. For $0\le r\le k$, define a bounded
$r$\nobreakdash-linear map $q_r$ by summing over the $r$-element subsets $S$ of $\{1,\dots,k\}$:
insert its $r$ arguments in the slots of $S$, in increasing order, and put $f_0$ in all remaining slots of $P$.
Multilinearity gives the finite identity
\[
A(f_0+h)=\sum_{r=0}^k q_r(h,\dots,h),
\qquad
\norm{q_r}\le\binom{k}{r}\norm P\norm{f_0}^{k-r}.
\]
The binomial coefficient in the bound is an ordinary nonnegative integer counting the summands.
With $q_r=0$ for $r>k$, this is a power series of infinite radius at every $f_0$.
Consequently $A$ is continuously polynomial, hence $C^\omega$ and $C^n$ for all
$n\in\N\cup\{\infty\}$. Equation~\eqref{eq:joint-action} makes the full bifunctor analytic on hom spaces,
and Proposition~\ref{prop:alt-spherical} shows that its values are nonarchimedean and spherically complete.
This proves the categorical statement.
\end{proof}

The retraction supplies the alternating values directly, with no division by $k!$.
The source spaces need no nonarchimedean hypothesis because the extension argument takes place in
the free presentation and in operator spaces with nonarchimedean targets. By contrast, the Banach target in the
counterexample theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:main}) admits no equivalent nonarchimedean norm.

\section{Analytic reflection beyond finite coordinates}\label{app:families}

This appendix supplements Section~\ref{sec:families-bundles}. We first prove the finite-coordinate reflection results, Lemma~\ref{fam:lem:diagonal} and Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-reflection}. We then isolate, for a general parameter space, the coefficient problem that the proof of Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-reflection} solves for $K^d$, and then prove the results on nonarchimedean $c_0$ parameters and ordinary $\ell^1$ parameters (Theorems~\ref{fam:thm:c0-reflection} and~\ref{fam:thm:l1-families}, Corollary~\ref{fam:cor:quotient-parameters}). Finally, \S\ref{fam:sec:tensors} gives a tensor criterion for universal analytic reflection: over a complete field, a Banach parameter space has this property exactly when the closed spans of pure tensor powers are ranges of projections whose norms grow at most exponentially (Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:tensor-analytic}).

\subsection{Finite-coordinate reflection}\label{app:ssec:finite-reflection}
This subsection proves Lemma~\ref{fam:lem:diagonal} and Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-reflection}.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{fam:lem:diagonal}]
For $n=0$ this follows by evaluating at $x_0$. Inductively, for each fixed $x$ and sufficiently small
nonzero $t\in K$, the vector
\[
 t^{-n}\left(f(x_0+tx)-\sum_{r<n}t^rp_r(x,\ldots,x)\right)
\]
belongs to $W$. It tends to $p_n(x,\ldots,x)$ as $t\to0$.
To see the convergence directly, choose $\rho>0$ and $M$ such that $\norm{p_r}\rho^r\le M$ for all $r$.
The norm of the remaining tail is at most
\[
 M\left(\frac{\norm{x}}{\rho}\right)^n
 \frac{\abs t\norm{x}/\rho}{1-\abs t\norm{x}/\rho}
\]
when $\abs t\norm{x}<\rho$. Nontriviality of the norm provides arbitrarily small nonzero $t$.
Closedness now proves the induction step.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-reflection}]
Identify $W$ with its image, use maximum-norm coordinates on $K^d$, and expand $jf$ at a parameter point.
Lemma~\ref{fam:lem:diagonal} shows that every homogeneous polynomial
$x\mapsto p_n(x,\ldots,x)$ has its values in $W$.
For $d\ge1$ and $n\ge1$, let $e_1,\ldots,e_d$ be the coordinate vectors. For each multi-index
$\alpha=(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_d)$ of total degree $n$, put
\[
 c_\alpha=
 \sum_{\substack{(i_1,\ldots,i_n)\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^n\\
                 \#\{r:i_r=j\}=\alpha_j\ (1\le j\le d)}}
 p_n(e_{i_1},\ldots,e_{i_n}).
\]
Then $p_n(x,\ldots,x)=\sum_{|\alpha|=n}c_\alpha x^\alpha$.
All $c_\alpha$ belong to $W$: pass to the algebraic quotient $Z/W$ and use polynomial uniqueness
over the infinite field $K$. This vector-valued uniqueness follows by applying scalar coordinate
functionals on the finite-dimensional span of the coefficients; no topology on the quotient is needed.

Choose, for each $\alpha$, the nondecreasing word $(i_1(\alpha),\ldots,i_n(\alpha))$ with those multiplicities,
and define the $W$\nobreakdash-valued multilinear map
\[
 q_n(x_1,\ldots,x_n)=
 \sum_{|\alpha|=n}c_\alpha\prod_{r=1}^n(x_r)_{i_r(\alpha)}.
\]
Its diagonal agrees with that of $p_n$. Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{fam:eq:finite-bound}
 \norm{q_n}\le\sum_{|\alpha|=n}\norm{c_\alpha}
 \le d^n\norm{p_n},
\end{equation}
because the original expansion has exactly $d^n$ ordered words.
The degree-zero coefficient is $f(x_0)$. The bounds give a common positive radius for the $q_n$.
Their partial sums have images under $j$ converging to $jf(x)$, so the isometry shows that they converge
to the already given vector $f(x)$ inside $W$. Completeness is therefore unnecessary.
The case $d=0$ is immediate, and continuous linear coordinates give the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The exact local coefficient problem}
The argument of Theorem~\ref{fam:thm:finite-reflection} identifies what remains to be proved for a general parameter space. Here $\gamma\colon U\to H_{XY}$, the action $A_{XY}$ and the closed isometric inclusion $j\colon W\hookrightarrow Z$ are as in Section~\ref{sec:families-bundles}.

\begin{proposition}\label{fam:prop:coefficient-criterion}
Let $\gamma$ be analytic near $x_0$ and choose an ambient expansion
\[
 jA_{XY}\gamma(x_0+h)=\sum_{n\ge0}p_n(h,\ldots,h).
\]
The induced map $A_{XY}\gamma$ is analytic at $x_0$ if and only if there are bounded $n$\nobreakdash-linear maps $q_n$
into $W$ and $r>0$ such that
\[
 jq_n(h,\ldots,h)=p_n(h,\ldots,h)\quad\text{for all }n,h,
 \qquad \sup_n\norm{q_n}r^n<\infty.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If the induced map is analytic at $x_0$, take an expansion there in $W$, apply $j$, and compare the two
expansions on every scalar line. Lemma~\ref{lem:unique} identifies their homogeneous diagonals.
Conversely, shrink $r$ to lie within the radius of the ambient expansion. The image under $j$ of the
partial sums of the proposed series converges to $jA_{XY}\gamma(x_0+h)$, because its homogeneous
diagonals agree term by term with the ambient series. Since $j$ is an isometry, the partial sums converge
inside $W$ to $A_{XY}\gamma(x_0+h)$.
\end{proof}

Admissibility on an open parameter domain means that this criterion holds at every point. For an incomplete
target, the existence of an expansion at a single point need not give analyticity on a neighborhood.
It is therefore not necessary that the chosen maps $p_n$ themselves take values in $W$ away from their
diagonals. Nor can the condition be replaced by ordinary derivative jets in positive characteristic:
Frobenius terms have vanishing derivatives but need not vanish as homogeneous power-series terms.
The relevant issue is a bounded multilinear representative for each homogeneous diagonal, with compatible
growth across the degrees.
